People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it's... it's a bit complicated, I suppose. I mean, I'm an executive assistant, right? My whole life, my whole career, is built on being proactive. Anticipating needs, organizing chaos, keeping the calendar spotless. I've always prided myself on that. But lately... and this is what really pushed me to make the appointment... everything just feels so incredibly heavy. And flat. It's not just work, though work is definitely suffering because I just can't seem to initiate anything anymore. It's the things I actually love doing, too. I used to go on these long photography walks on the weekends, you know, just me and my camera, capturing the light hitting the buildings downtown or the trees in the park. It was my way of recharging. And I mentor some kids at the local community center on Tuesday evenings. I've been doing that for three years. But now... I just don't have the drive for any of it. The thought of picking up my camera or even just updating my own personal calendar feels like trying to move a mountain. I just sit there. The things that usually make me feel like *me*, they just don't spark anything anymore. I've just completely stopped initiating any of it, and it's starting to really worry me because this just isn't who I am.

Well, let me think... it hasn't just been a sudden thing, you know? It's been creeping up on me. I'd say it's been going on for about a year and three months now. And even though things have settled down a bit with him now, that heavy, flat feeling... it just never went away. It's been about fifteen months of just feeling like my battery is permanently at one percent.

You know, I've really been racking my brain trying to think if there was ever a time before this, but honestly... no. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has a bad week here and there, right? Like back in, oh, it must have been 2012, we had this massive corporate merger at my old firm, and I was pulling sixty, maybe seventy-hour weeks just keeping my VP's schedule from completely imploding. I was exhausted, absolutely bone-tired. I remember I'd come home and just crash on the sofa with my shoes still on, barely able to microwave a dinner. 

But even then, even when I was that physically drained, I never lost my *drive*. I still wanted to get up the next day and tackle the inbox, you know? I still looked forward to my photography walks on Sunday mornings, even if I was a little sleepy doing them. I've always been the kind of person who thrives on momentum. In my twenties, in my thirties, I was always the one organizing the group trips, making the itineraries, the one who was just always... on. I was the reliable one. 

So this feeling, this complete lack of wanting to do *anything*, this heavy flatness where even opening my personal email feels like a monumental chore... it's completely uncharted territory for me. It's so strange, because I keep waiting to just snap out of it, to wake up and be my old proactive self again, the guy who has the whole week planned out by Sunday afternoon, but it just hasn't happened. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the very first time in my forty-six years I've ever experienced anything remotely like this. It's totally new, and frankly, it's exhausting in a completely different way than just being overworked.

It's so strange, because I keep waiting to just snap out of it, to wake up and be my old proactive self again, the guy who has the whole week planned out by Sunday afternoon, but it just hasn't happened. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the very first time in my forty-six years I've ever experienced anything remotely like this. It's totally new, and frankly, it's exhausting in a completely different way than just being overworked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my sleep has been... it's been really strange, actually. Completely out of whack. I mean, usually, I'm a solid seven-to-eight-hours-a-night kind of guy. You have to be, right? When you're managing a VP's schedule and coordinating flights across three different time zones, you can't be running on fumes. I've always had a very strict nighttime routine—chamomile tea at nine, screens off by ten, in bed by ten-thirty. It worked like a charm for years. 

But over this past year, especially since all that stuff with my brother-in-law started, it's just gone completely out the window. And it's not even that I'm tossing and turning all night, though there's some of that, sure. It's more like... there have been these nights, and it happens more often than I'd like to admit, where I'll go to bed, fall asleep, and then boom. I'm wide awake at, like, 3:00 AM. I mean, pitch black outside, the whole neighborhood is dead silent, the dog is snoring on the rug, and my eyes just pop open. I'll have only slept for maybe two or three hours total. 

And the weirdest part isn't even waking up that early. The weirdest part is how I feel when it happens. You'd think I'd be exhausted, right? Groggy, desperate to go back to sleep. But no. I wake up before dawn, and I feel completely wired. Like, I feel this sudden, intense urge to just get up and start working. I'll lie there for a minute, and my brain will just start buzzing with all the things I need to do. I'll think, 'Oh, I should really respond to those emails from the London office right now,' or 'I need to completely reorganize the Q3 appointment lists.' It's like my body thinks three hours is a full night's rest and it's time to tackle the day. I don't feel tired at all in those moments. I just feel this bizarre, restless energy to be productive in the middle of the night, which makes absolutely no sense because during the day, like I said, I can barely muster the energy to open my own personal email. It's incredibly frustrating, and it just throws my whole rhythm off completely. I'll just sit there in the dark, wide awake, wondering why my brain is telling me to draft memos at three in the morning instead of letting me sleep.

I was practically running a second full-time job just managing his health crisis. And it was just so draining, emotionally and logistically. I think that's when I first started noticing that I didn't want to pick up my camera anymore. It was like I was using up all my executive function, all my drive, just keeping his life organized, and I had absolutely nothing left for my own. And even though things have settled down a bit with him now, that heavy, flat feeling... it just never went away. It's been about fifteen months of just feeling like my battery is permanently at one percent.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about fifteen months ago, have you ever had any other periods in your life where you felt this down or lost your motivation?\n\nPatient: You know, I've really been racking my brain trying to think if there was ever a time before this, but honestly... no. Not like this. I mean, sure, everyone has a bad week here and there, right? Like back in, oh, it must have been 2012, we had this massive corporate merger at my old firm, and I was pulling sixty, maybe seventy-hour weeks just keeping my VP's schedule from completely imploding. I was exhausted, absolutely bone-tired. I remember I'd come home and just crash on the sofa with my shoes still on, barely able to microwave a dinner. \n\nBut even then, even when I was that physically drained, I never lost my *drive*. I still wanted to get up the next day and tackle the inbox, you know? I still looked forward to my photography walks on Sunday mornings, even if I was a little sleepy doing them. I've always been the kind of person who thrives on momentum. In my twenties, in my thirties, I was always the one organizing the group trips, making the itineraries, the one who was just always... on. I was the reliable one. \n\nSo this feeling, this complete lack of wanting to do *anything*, this heavy flatness where even opening my personal email feels like a monumental chore... it's completely uncharted territory for me. It's so strange, because I keep waiting to just snap out of it, to wake up and be my old proactive self again, the guy who has the whole week planned out by Sunday afternoon, but it just hasn't happened. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the very first time in my forty-six years I've ever experienced anything remotely like this. It's totally new, and frankly, it's exhausting in a completely different way than just being overworked.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been during this past year or so?\n\nPatient: Well, my sleep has been... it's been really strange, actually. Completely out of whack. I mean, usually, I'm a solid seven-to-eight-hours-a-night kind of guy. You have to be, right? When you're managing a VP's schedule and coordinating flights across three different time zones, you can't be running on fumes. I've always had a very strict nighttime routine—chamomile tea at nine, screens off by ten, in bed by ten-thirty. It worked like a charm for years. \n\nBut over this past year, especially since all that stuff with my brother-in-law started, it's just gone completely out the window. And it's not even that I'm tossing and turning all night, though there's some of that, sure. It's more like... there have been these nights, and it happens more often than I'd like to admit, where I'll go to bed, fall asleep, and then boom. I'm wide awake at, like, 3:00 AM. I mean, pitch black outside, the whole neighborhood is dead silent, the dog is snoring on the rug, and my eyes just pop open. I'll have only slept for maybe two or three hours total. \n\nAnd the weirdest part isn't even waking up that early. The weirdest part is how I feel when it happens. You'd think I'd be exhausted, right? Groggy, desperate to go back to sleep. But no. I wake up before dawn, and I feel completely wired. Like, I feel this sudden, intense urge to just get up and start working. I'll lie there for a minute, and my brain will just start buzzing with all the things I need to do. I'll think, 'Oh, I should really respond to those emails from the London office right now,' or 'I need to completely reorganize the Q3 appointment lists.' It's like my body thinks three hours is a full night's rest and it's time to tackle the day. I don't feel tired at all in those moments. I just feel this bizarre, restless energy to be productive in the middle of the night, which makes absolutely no sense because during the day, like I said, I can barely muster the energy to open my own personal email. It's incredibly frustrating, and it just throws my whole rhythm off completely. I'll just sit there in the dark, wide awake, wondering why my brain is telling me to draft memos at three in the morning instead of letting me sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes and no. It's not like I'm bouncing off the walls or anything, but there are definitely these stretches... these periods where I just feel, I don't know, unusually upbeat? Like everything is just clicking into place perfectly. I'll be at work, and I'll have this absolute certainty that I'm on top of every single detail. The calendar is flawless, the travel arrangements are seamless, and I just feel this incredible momentum, like I can anticipate what my VP needs before he even knows he needs it. It's a great feeling, honestly, feeling that sharp and capable, like all the gears in my head are turning exactly right. 

But the thing is, during those same stretches, it's like... it's like a switch can flip over the smallest thing. If someone throws a wrench in the works—say, someone from accounting reschedules a meeting by fifteen minutes, or the caterer is late with the lunch order—I don't just get annoyed. I get... sharp. I'll snap at people in a way that honestly surprises me. I'll hear the tone of my own voice on a phone call and think, 'Whoa, where did that come from?' Because normally, I'm the diplomat, right? You have to be in my job. You smooth things over, you find workarounds. But during these times when I'm feeling so incredibly dialed in, any little disruption just feels like a personal affront, and I can be really irritable, really short with people who are just trying to do their jobs. It's like I have zero tolerance for anything that breaks that perfect rhythm I feel like I'm in. And then I have to go back later and do damage control, apologizing for being so curt, which is just... it's so out of character for me, but in the moment, the frustration is just so intense and immediate. So, yeah, I guess there are these times of feeling really, really good, but it comes with this weird, prickly edge if things don't go exactly my way.

How many days... you know, that's actually a bit tricky to answer with just a simple number, because... well, it's more of a larger pattern, if that makes sense. It's exhausting in its own way, just waiting for the next time I flip into that mode.

It's exhausting in its own way, just waiting for the next time I flip into that mode.\n\n[Last Question]\n```json\n{\"intent\": \"Determine the duration of each distinct period of elevated mood to evaluate if it meets the criteria for a hypomanic episode\", \"question\": \"\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You mean like, how many days in a row does one single stretch last before it stops? Gosh, I... I really couldn't tell you an exact number. It's not like I'm marking it down on my Outlook calendar alongside everything else, you know? 

I mean, I track everything for my VP. I know exactly when his dentist appointments are, I know how many days he's spending in the Chicago office versus the Seattle office, down to the absolute minute. My entire professional life is built on knowing durations and timelines. But for myself? For these specific moods? It all just kind of blends together into this big blur of productivity and irritability. 

I'll be in the thick of it, feeling like I'm orchestrating this beautiful, flawless symphony of schedules and logistics, and time just sort of loses its meaning. Is it three days? Is it seven days? I honestly have no idea. Sometimes I think maybe it's just a really intense long weekend where I'm buzzing and getting the house completely spotless, and other times it feels like I've been running on that high-octane fuel at the office for what feels like weeks. Because when you're in it, you're just *in* it. You're answering those emails at 3:00 AM, you're snapping at the caterer on a Tuesday, you're completely overhauling the digital filing system on a Thursday. You don't stop to think, 'Okay, this is day four of feeling invincible.' 

I've never actually stopped to count the days from when that switch flips on to when it finally flips off and I go back to feeling... whatever normal is supposed to be. It's incredibly frustrating because I am usually such a details guy. I pride myself on having the data for everything. But with this? I really haven't kept track of the actual length of each individual time it happens. I just know it keeps coming back.

I'll have only slept for maybe two or three hours total. \n\nAnd the weirdest part isn't even waking up that early. The weirdest part is how I feel when it happens. You'd think I'd be exhausted, right? Groggy, desperate to go back to sleep. But no. I wake up before dawn, and I feel completely wired. Like, I feel this sudden, intense urge to just get up and start working. I'll lie there for a minute, and my brain will just start buzzing with all the things I need to do. Oh, you mean like... did it completely blow up my life or put me in the hospital or something? No, no, nothing extreme like that. I haven't lost my job or done anything completely off the rails. My work has stayed intact overall, thankfully. 

But... well, it's funny you use the word disruption, because even though the building isn't burning down, there is definitely a disruption in how I operate. Like I was saying earlier about how I pride myself on all those little logistics? During these times, I've actually missed some calendar details. And for me, in my role, that is just... it's huge. It's practically a crisis. I'm the guy who normally calculates time zone differences in my head for international flights without blinking, but suddenly I'm looking at a Tuesday schedule and realizing I completely glossed over a conflict between a board meeting and a vendor call. It really shakes my confidence. I find myself having to go back and double-check tasks that used to be completely automatic. Like, muscle-memory automatic. I'll stare at my outbox thinking, 'Did I actually send that confirmation email, or did I just think about sending it really fast?' So I end up spending all this extra time just verifying my own work, which kind of defeats the purpose of feeling so productive in the first place.

And it's not just at the office, either. It definitely bleeds over into my personal life. I've actually pulled back quite a bit from things I usually love doing. Like my photography outings. I used to go out a lot with this local group, doing urban landscape stuff—getting up early to catch the light on the architecture downtown, which requires a lot of patience and stillness. But lately, I just haven't been going. I've also been pulling back from this mentoring program I do for young professionals entering administrative roles. I usually find it so rewarding, but I've been canceling on my mentee. 

I just... I don't feel as present or reliable right now, if that makes sense. When my mind is either going a million miles an hour or I've got that prickly, irritable edge I mentioned, I just can't sit still enough to wait for the perfect camera shot. And I certainly don't have the patience to sit across a coffee table from a twenty-two-year-old and give them thoughtful career advice. I feel like my brain is somewhere else entirely. So rather than do a bad job or snap at someone, I just withdraw. I just stay home. So, no, no hospitals or anything catastrophic, but it's definitely messing with my normal routine in ways that really bother me.

Alcohol or recreational... oh, no. No, definitely not. I mean, if you're asking if I'm taking something that's making me feel this way, the answer is absolutely not. I am incredibly strict about that sort of thing.

So, no, I'm not self-medicating or partying or taking anything to keep me awake or make me feel invincible. I almost wish it were that simple, honestly, because then I could just stop taking whatever it is and go back to normal. But no, there are no substances involved here at all. Heard or seen things... well. Gosh, that’s a bit of an intimidating question, isn't it? I haven't seen anything, no. No visions or shadows or anything dramatic out of a movie. But... as far as hearing things... yes. Actually, yes. And it's been incredibly unsettling for me, to be perfectly honest. 

It usually happens when I'm completely alone, which is rare enough in my line of work, but say I'm sitting at my desk. I have this workspace right in the alcove outside the executive suite, and usually, I can hear people approaching because the carpet out there is a bit thin over the floorboards. But sometimes, especially when I'm in one of these weird, wired moods we were just talking about, I'll be sitting there typing away, and I will hear my name. Just spoken out loud. Or, even more specifically, I'll hear this short, sharp comment, like someone leaning right over my shoulder and saying, 'fix the schedule.' Just like that. 'Fix the schedule.' 

And it is so clear. It is as clear as you and I talking right now. It's not a muffled sound from the hallway or the HVAC system kicking on, which I know can sometimes sound like murmuring. No, this sounds exactly like a person is standing right beside me. So, of course, my immediate reaction is to stop what I'm doing, and I physically spin my chair around to answer them, thinking it's my boss or one of the junior coordinators needing something urgent. But... there’s absolutely no one there. The hallway is completely empty. 

It happens in the car, too. I’ll be driving back from an appointment—like the other day, I was driving back from scouting a potential off-site venue for our spring summit, this boutique hotel with a frankly abysmal valet situation—and I was completely alone in my sedan. The windows were rolled up, the radio was entirely off because I needed the quiet to mentally calculate the catering budget, and suddenly I heard that same kind of voice right next to my ear. I actually gasped and swerved a little bit. I looked in the passenger seat, I checked the rearview mirror thinking somehow someone had gotten into the back seat of my car without me knowing... but it was just me. Just me and the empty car. 

It really, really throws me off. Because, as I've said, I rely so much on my perception and my accuracy to do my job well, and having your own ears play tricks on you like that when you're just trying to drive or update a calendar... it's just exhausting. It makes me second-guess my own reality, which is the absolute last thing I have time for right now.

Physically, on paper, I am completely, annoyingly healthy. Which is great, obviously, I know I shouldn't complain about being in good physical health, but it just leaves me right back where I started with all of... this. So, no, there's no medical explanation that we know of.\n\nDoctor: During any of these periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people couldn't?\n\nPatient: Heard or seen things... well. Gosh, that’s a bit of an intimidating question, isn't it? I haven't seen anything, no. No visions or shadows or anything dramatic out of a movie. But... as far as hearing things... yes. Actually, yes. And it's been incredibly unsettling for me, to be perfectly honest. \n\nIt usually happens when I'm completely alone, which is rare enough in my line of work, but say I'm sitting at my desk. I have this workspace right in the alcove outside the executive suite, and usually, I can hear people approaching because the carpet out there is a bit thin over the floorboards. But sometimes, especially when I'm in one of these weird, wired moods we were just talking about, I'll be sitting there typing away, and I will hear my name. Just spoken out loud. Or, even more specifically, I'll hear this short, sharp comment, like someone leaning right over my shoulder and saying, 'fix the schedule.' Just like that. 'Fix the schedule.' \n\nAnd it is so clear. It is as clear as you and I talking right now. It's not a muffled sound from the hallway or the HVAC system kicking on, which I know can sometimes sound like murmuring. No, this sounds exactly like a person is standing right beside me. So, of course, my immediate reaction is to stop what I'm doing, and I physically spin my chair around to answer them, thinking it's my boss or one of the junior coordinators needing something urgent. But... there’s absolutely no one there. The hallway is completely empty. \n\nIt happens in the car, too. I’ll be driving back from an appointment—like the other day, I was driving back from scouting a potential off-site venue for our spring summit, this boutique hotel with a frankly abysmal valet situation—and I was completely alone in my sedan. The windows were rolled up, the radio was entirely off because I needed the quiet to mentally calculate the catering budget, and suddenly I heard that same kind of voice right next to my ear. I actually gasped and swerved a little bit. I looked in the passenger seat, I checked the rearview mirror thinking somehow someone had gotten into the back seat of my car without me knowing... but it was just me. Just me and the empty car. \n\nIt really, really throws me off. Because, as I've said, I rely so much on my perception and my accuracy to do my job well, and having your own ears play tricks on you like that when you're just trying to drive or update a calendar... it's just exhausting. It makes me second-guess my own reality, which is the absolute last thing I have time for right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Have I... heard those things when my mood is normal? You mean when I'm not buzzing around on four hours of sleep or feeling like I can conquer the corporate world in a single afternoon? 

Yes. Yes, actually, I have. And that's what makes it so incredibly frustrating, because I used to think, 'Okay, I'm just overtired, I'm just overworked, I'm running on adrenaline and caffeine fumes'—even though I don't drink caffeine after 2:00 PM, as I mentioned, but you know what I mean. I thought it was just a byproduct of being so revved up. 

But there have definitely been times—weeks, actually—where I felt completely fine. Completely level. I was sleeping my normal seven hours, I wasn't pacing the floors, I wasn't feeling that weird electric hum in my chest. Just a totally normal, calm week. And yet... it still happened. 

I remember specifically, it was early last fall. Things at work were actually quite slow, which is rare. The VP was on vacation in Tuscany, so my schedule was practically empty. I was just doing routine filing, updating some contacts, feeling very relaxed. And for more than two weeks during that quiet period, I was still hearing my name called out when no one was there. Just... 'hey,' or my name, clear as day. 

And it wasn't just the voices, either. During that same calm period, I had this overwhelming, unshakable feeling that the few emails I was getting from the other admins—just totally mundane things like, 'Did you restock the printer toner?' or 'Is the conference room free at three?'—I was utterly convinced they were coded tests. Like they were trying to catch me slipping up, or testing my loyalty to the company, or secretly assessing my competence while the boss was away. I spent hours analyzing this one email about coffee filters, trying to decode what the sender was *really* asking me. 

And I wasn't manic. I wasn't depressed. I was just sitting there at my desk, feeling completely physically normal, but having to constantly fight off these voices and these intense, paranoid thoughts about ordinary office communication. So, yes, it definitely happens even when my mood is perfectly fine. Which, honestly, is almost scarier, because I can't just blame it on being sleep-deprived or overly energized. It's just... there.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. But lately... and this is what really pushed me to make the appointment... everything just feels so incredibly heavy. And flat. It's not just work, though work is definitely suffering because I just can't seem to initiate anything anymore. It's the things I actually love doing, too. I used to go on these long photography walks on the weekends, you know, just me and my camera, capturing the light hitting the buildings downtown or the trees in the park.
